# Human check labelling sheet

Seed: 20261020. Drawn from the judge study's sample: 15 fact items and 15 claim items, for a person to label by hand.

Label each item on its `LABEL` line, after the colon. A fact item is `stated` or `not stated`: does the answer state the Required Fact, in any wording? A claim item is `supported`, `unsupported` or `contradicted`: how do the cited Sources relate to the claim? Its context (heading, table header) is there to read the claim by, not to be judged. Label every fact item, then the claim items in order. Leave a label blank to come back to it; drawing the sheet again keeps every label written here.

Label from each item alone. Until every item here is labelled, don't open the judge study's `sheet.md`, `label-reasons.md` or `sample.json`, or its verdicts or score: they hold the labels this sheet checks.

## Fact items

### 1. F-c8534842a1

**Required Fact**

> Meta's Reality Labs revenue increased 3% in 2025 compared to 2024.

**Answer**

> Meta’s revenue grew in both fiscal years, rising from **$164,501 million in FY2024** to **$200,966 million in FY2025**; the FY2025 filing text describes this as an increase of **22%**, driven by advertising revenue. [S4] [S8] [S14] The quarterly figures show growth accelerating through FY2025: year-over-year revenue growth was 16.1% in Q1, 21.6% in Q2 and 26.2% in Q3, before moderating to a derived 23.8% in Q4. [S5] [S1] [S6] [S2] [S7] [S3] [S8] [S4]
>
> The comparison uses Meta’s **10-K for FY2024, report period December 31, 2024**, and **10-K for FY2025, report period December 31, 2025**; quarterly detail comes from 10-Qs for Q1–Q3 of each fiscal year. [S1] [S2] [S3] [S4] [S5] [S6] [S7] [S8]
>
> ### Revenue trajectory
>
> | Fiscal period | Revenue | Year-over-year change |
> |---|---:|---:|
> | FY2024 | $164,501 million | — [S4] |
> | FY2025 | $200,966 million | +22.2% (derived, as reported in Financial Facts) [S8] [S4] |
>
> Quarterly revenue increased from **$36,455 million in Q1 FY2024** to **$42,314 million in Q1 FY2025** (+16.1%); from **$39,071 million** to **$47,516 million** in Q2 (+21.6%); and from **$40,589 million** to **$51,242 million** in Q3 (+26.2%). [S1] [S5] [S2] [S6] [S3] [S7] Q4 FY2024 revenue was **$48,386 million**, derived from FY2024 revenue less Q1–Q3; Q4 FY2025 was **$59,894 million**, derived the same way from FY2025 revenue. The Q4 year-over-year increase of 23.8% is also derived from those quarterly figures. [S4] [S1] [S2] [S3] [S8] [S5] [S6] [S7]
>
> ### What drove the change
>
> Meta’s FY2025 filing text says advertising revenue increased **$35.54 billion, or 22%**, due to increases in ad impressions and average price per ad. Impressions rose 12% and average price per ad rose 9% year over year; Meta linked the latter to increased advertising demand, which it believes is mostly due to ongoing improvements to ad targeting and measurement tools. Impressions grew in all regions, especially Asia-Pacific, driven by increases in users and engagement. [S14]
>
> Family of Apps (FoA) revenue increased **$36.40 billion, or 22%**, while Reality Labs (RL) revenue increased **$61 million, or 3%**. FoA’s increase was almost entirely driven by advertising; its other revenue rose $862 million, or 50%, mostly from WhatsApp paid messaging and Meta Verified subscriptions. RL’s modest increase reflected higher AI-glasses sales, partly offset by lower Meta Quest sales. [S14]
>
> **Coverage:** No Coverage Gaps are listed. The filings cover FY2024 and FY2025, but do not provide revenue after FY2025.

### 2. F-e63281185b

**Required Fact**

> UnitedHealth says recently acquired or non-integrated businesses have brought heightened cybersecurity vulnerabilities.

**Answer**

> UnitedHealth’s latest annual report—the **FY2024 Form 10-K, report period December 31, 2024**—describes cybersecurity risks involving attacks on its own systems and third-party vendors, exposure or misuse of sensitive information, and disruption to business operations. It says these events could cause financial loss, liability, regulatory consequences and reputational harm; the Q1, Q2 and Q3 FY2025 Forms 10-Q, for report periods March 31, June 30 and September 30, 2025, each state that there were no material changes to the 2024 10-K risk factors. [S1][S2][S3][S4]
>
> ### Cybersecurity risks described
>
> - **Attacks and security incidents:** UnitedHealth says it is regularly targeted by attempted cyberattacks and other security threats, and has previously experienced compromises of its systems or information, including information held on its behalf by third parties. It specifically notes that its recently acquired Change Healthcare business was subject to a cyberattack in 2024 involving protected health information or personally identifiable information. [S1]
>
> - **Evolving and hard-to-detect threats:** The company warns that techniques for unauthorized access, service disruption or sabotage change frequently and are becoming more sophisticated, in part through evolving AI/ML technologies, including generative AI. It may be unable to anticipate those techniques, detect incidents promptly or implement adequate preventive measures. [S1]
>
> - **Operational disruption and compromised information:** Threat actors could penetrate security controls, disrupt or shut down systems and operations, or access, misappropriate or otherwise compromise protected personal information and proprietary or confidential information, including information belonging to third parties. The filing also identifies viruses, ransomware and other malware, as well as vulnerabilities in hardware, software or applications developed or procured from third parties. [S1]
>
> - **Other paths to incidents:** The company identifies financial fraud schemes, misplaced or lost data, errors and malicious social engineering as risks to data, financial accounts and operations. It also says recently acquired or non-integrated businesses can create heightened vulnerabilities, and that third-party vendors processing, storing or transmitting data face similar risks outside UnitedHealth’s direct oversight and control. [S1]
>
> - **Consequences and response:** A security incident could interrupt operations or services, lead to customer losses, increase remediation and operating costs, or expose the company to liability, litigation and regulatory penalties. Unauthorized disclosure could also result in financial or medical identity theft, misuse or loss of information, and damage to the company’s brand and reputation. UnitedHealth says it has detection, containment and response programs, employee security-awareness training, and business-continuation and resiliency plans that it maintains, updates and tests; it cautions that remediation may not succeed. [S1]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> Coverage Gaps: **None.** The filings do not provide a separate, updated cybersecurity risk-factor discussion in the FY2025 quarterly reports; they state that there were no material changes to the FY2024 10-K risk factors. [S2][S3][S4]

### 3. F-302cfcc50e

**Required Fact**

> Merck says increased utilization of the 340B program is having a negative impact on its performance.

**Answer**

> Merck faces regulatory risks from Medicare price setting and rebate changes, drug and vaccine approval requirements, and evolving compliance obligations; its filing specifically identifies potential effects on Keytruda, Januvia, Janumet and Janumet XR. [S1] **Not in the Corpus: Bristol Myers Squibb is not in the Filings provided, so this answer does not cover it.**
>
> ## Merck
>
> The assessment below uses Merck’s **10-K, FY2024, report period December 31, 2024**, specifically Item 1A, Risk Factors. [S1]
>
> ### Pricing, reimbursement and mandatory discounts
> - **Inflation Reduction Act (IRA):** Merck identifies inflation-related financial penalties, Medicare Part D redesign requiring manufacturers to bear more liability beginning in 2025, and government price setting for selected Part D drugs starting in 2026 and Part B drugs starting in 2028. Government price setting may also affect private-market pricing. [S1]
> - **Product-specific exposure:** Januvia’s government-set price becomes effective January 1, 2026. HHS selected Janumet and Janumet XR on January 17, 2025, for prices effective January 1, 2027. Merck expects HHS to select Keytruda in 2026, with pricing effective January 1, 2028, and expects U.S. Keytruda sales to decline afterward. These are expectations stated in the filing, not assured outcomes. [S1]
> - **Medicaid and other programs:** The American Rescue Plan Act removed the Medicaid rebate cap beginning January 2024, potentially requiring rebates exceeding receipts on particular products. Increased use of the **340B Federal Drug Discount Program** and restrictions on identifying inappropriate discounts are negatively affecting performance. Merck also expects states to increasingly use **Prescription Drug Affordability Boards**, including affordability reviews and upper payment limits. [S1]
>
> ### Approvals, vaccine policy and manufacturing
> - Merck cannot market products without obtaining and maintaining approvals in each jurisdiction. The FDA and authorities in the EU, Japan and China can require additional testing, delay or withhold approvals, or prevent distribution and sale. [S1]
> - Potential HHS changes could make FDA drug and vaccine approvals and CDC vaccine recommendations more difficult. Eliminating FDA drug user-fee programs could significantly delay facility inspections and new-product approvals; Merck says it is too early to assess which proposed changes will occur. [S1]
> - Failure to comply with **Current Good Manufacturing Practices** can cause shutdowns, shortages and delays. Human vaccine candidates require a biologics license application, and FDA approval is generally required for release of each manufactured commercial human vaccine lot. [S1]
> - After approval, re-reviews, safety findings, labeling changes, recalls and withdrawal of accelerated-approval indications can reduce demand or prevent continued sales. [S1]
>
> ### Broader compliance exposure
> Merck identifies the **Foreign Corrupt Practices Act**, privacy requirements in the EU, U.S. and China, local-manufacturing mandates, and reporting of payments to health care professionals as compliance risks. China’s anti-corruption inspections and audits could increase administrative burdens and negatively affect sales. [S1] Sustainability requirements include the EU’s **Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive**, California emissions and climate-risk reporting laws, and potential SEC climate reporting requirements, described as stayed in the filing. [S1]
>
> ## Coverage
>
> **Not in the Corpus: Bristol Myers Squibb is not in the Filings provided, so this answer does not cover it.** Merck coverage is limited to one FY2024 10-K snapshot; the supplied excerpt does not establish subsequent regulatory developments or quantify the aggregate financial impact of these risks. [S1]

### 4. F-7754d69f34

**Required Fact**

> JPMorgan lists market risks, including the effects of changes in interest rates and credit spreads, among its principal risk factors.

**Answer**

> Apple, Tesla and JPMorgan Chase each identify risks that could materially harm their businesses, but their exposures differ: Apple highlights global product demand, supply-chain concentration, tariffs and technology-market regulation; Tesla emphasizes executing product and manufacturing ramps, supplier availability, vehicle and energy-product risks, and its dependence on key personnel; JPMorgan’s risk profile centers on financial regulation, market and credit conditions, liquidity and capital requirements, and operational and cyber resilience. [S1] [S3] [S4] These filings cover different periods—Apple’s 10-K for FY2025, year ended 2025-09-27, and Tesla’s and JPMorgan Chase’s 10-Ks for FY2025, each year ended 2025-12-31—so this is not a same-period comparison. [S1] [S3] [S4]
>
> ### Apple
>
> Apple identifies dependence on global and regional economic conditions as a material risk: slow growth or recession, inflation, tighter credit, higher interest rates and currency fluctuations can weaken consumer confidence and spending. [S1] Its international sales represent a majority of net sales, and a majority of supplier facilities—including manufacturing and assembly sites—are outside the U.S., increasing exposure to disruption across its global supply chain. [S1]
>
> Trade measures are a specific current concern: beginning in Q2 2025, new U.S. tariffs were announced, including additional tariffs on imports from China, India, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan, Vietnam and the EU; other countries imposed or threatened reciprocal tariffs. [S1] Apple also points to the U.S. Department of Commerce’s Section 232 investigation into imports of semiconductors, semiconductor manufacturing equipment and derivative products containing semiconductors; the eventual impact remains uncertain. [S1]
>
> Operationally, Apple relies on outsourcing partners, primarily in China mainland, India, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan and Vietnam, and partners in Asia for final assembly of substantially all hardware products; it also relies on single-source suppliers for components. [S1] The company cites risks from shortages and price increases, defects, product-transition execution and demand forecasting. [S1] Regulatory exposure includes EU Digital Markets Act changes to iOS, iPadOS, the App Store and Safari, as well as U.S. changes to developer communications and a court order concerning commissions or fees on certain purchases. [S1] Apple’s Q1 FY2026 10-Q says there were no material changes to its risk factors since the FY2025 10-K. [S2]
>
> ### Tesla
>
> Tesla’s central execution risk is developing, launching and ramping products and services on time and at planned cost, quality and output. Its filing specifically names driver assistance and autonomous driving, mass-market vehicles including Cybercab, Bots including Optimus, energy storage products and Solar Roof. [S3] It says future success depends on advancing AI capabilities, building efficient manufacturing and supply chains, and hiring and training skilled employees. [S3]
>
> Tesla also faces supply risks from thousands of globally purchased parts and hundreds of suppliers, including single-source direct suppliers. It says 2025 U.S. trade-policy changes, including heightened import tariffs and retaliatory measures, have affected supply-chain costs and may affect component availability. [S3] Further exposures include lithium-ion cell production and safety, product liability, and self-insurance for vehicle product-liability exposure in most jurisdictions. [S3] The filing also identifies dependence on CEO Elon Musk and the challenge of attracting and retaining qualified personnel. [S3]
>
> ### JPMorgan Chase
>
> JPMorgan’s filing foregrounds extensive supervision and regulation: legal or supervisory changes can require it to limit products, raise prices, reduce market-making liquidity, pay higher taxes, dispose of assets or forgo opportunities; noncompliance can bring enforcement actions, penalties, litigation or reputational harm. [S4] Its consumer business is exposed to unemployment, inflation, interest-rate changes, housing and vehicle collateral values, and consumer debt, which can raise delinquencies, credit-loss allowances and net charge-offs. [S4]
>
> Other material exposures include constrained liquidity, credit-rating downgrades that raise funding costs or trigger collateral requirements, and regulatory capital requirements that can limit dividends, share repurchases or business activity. [S4] Operationally, JPMorgan reports past system disruptions affecting customers, including inability to access accounts, exfiltration of personal data and duplicative transactions; it says future cyber breaches are inevitable and could cause losses, investigations, penalties and reputational harm. [S4]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> Coverage gap: **Periods differ**: the Companies’ latest Filings here cover different periods (Apple: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-09-27; Tesla: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-12-31; JPMorgan Chase: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-12-31), so the comparison is not of the same period. The sources describe risk factors, not their likelihood, relative financial magnitude or a common quantitative ranking.

### 5. F-95c6681623

**Required Fact**

> Merck says increased utilization of the 340B program is having a negative impact on its performance.

**Answer**

> Merck's FY2024 10-K lists regulatory risks centred on the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) price-setting program, Medicaid rebate changes, FDA and other approval dependencies, and global compliance laws [S1]. Bristol Myers Squibb is not in the Filings provided, so this answer does not cover it.
>
> ## Merck & Co Inc (MRK) – 10-K, FY2024, report period 2024-12-31
>
> **IRA and U.S. pricing regulation**
> - The IRA, passed in 2022, creates financial penalties for drugs whose prices rise faster than inflation. It also redesigns Medicare Part D so manufacturers bear more liability, which took effect in 2025. It adds government price setting for certain Part D drugs from 2026 and Part B drugs from 2028 [S1].
> - HHS selected Januvia in 2023, and a government-set price becomes effective January 1, 2026. On January 17, 2025, HHS announced that Janumet and Janumet XR were selected, effective January 1, 2027 [S1].
> - Merck expects HHS to include Keytruda in a subsequent selection in 2026, with a price effective January 1, 2028. It expects U.S. Keytruda sales to decline after that [S1]. Keytruda was 46% of total sales in 2024 [S1].
> - Government price setting may also affect pricing in the private market [S1].
>
> **Medicaid, 340B and state actions**
> - The American Rescue Plan Act eliminated the statutory cap on Medicaid rebates from January 2024. Merck may have to pay state Medicaid programs more in rebates than it received on sales of some products, particularly those with high Medicaid utilization [S1].
> - Increased use of the 340B Federal Drug Discount Program, and restrictions on identifying inappropriate discounts, are hurting performance [S1].
> - Merck expects states to move toward tools such as Prescription Drug Affordability Boards, which can set upper payment limits and might select its products for review [S1].
>
> **FDA and approval risk**
> - Products cannot be marketed without approval from the FDA and from foreign authorities, including in the EU, Japan and China. These authorities can require more testing or delay or withhold approval [S1].
> - Possible HHS changes could make it harder for the FDA to approve drugs and vaccines, and for the CDC to issue or maintain vaccine recommendations. If FDA drug user fee programs were eliminated, facility inspections and approvals could be significantly delayed [S1].
> - After approval, risks include Phase 4 trial results, re-review, recalls, loss of marketing approval, and withdrawal of indications granted under accelerated approval. Authorities such as the FDA, EMA, Japan's PMDA and China's NMPA have increased their focus on safety [S1].
>
> **Compliance, trade and tax**
> - Laws that could raise compliance costs include the U.S. Foreign Corrupt Practices Act, data privacy rules in the EU, U.S. and China, local-manufacturing mandates, payment-reporting rules for health care professionals, and the EU's CSRD [S1].
> - The 10-K also cites California's GHG reporting law and the SEC climate rules, which are currently stayed [S1].
> - China's anti-corruption campaign, with more inspections and audits, could hurt sales there [S1].
> - The U.S. government has announced plans to significantly increase tariffs, particularly on Canada and Mexico, and has already raised tariffs on China. Merck says it is too early to assess the effect [S1].
> - The IRS is examining the 2017 and 2018 returns, including the TCJA one-time transition tax, and the 2021 and 2022 returns. Merck also notes OECD Base Erosion and Profit Shifting disclosure requirements and the OECD two-pillar solution [S1].
> - Merck treats the 2021 Organon spin-off as tax-free. If that position fails, significant federal tax liabilities could follow [S1].
>
> **Legal exposure**
> - Merck cites litigation involving Gardasil, along with FCPA obligations and antitrust and pricing matters [S1]. It self-insures substantially all product liability risk [S1].
>
> ## Coverage
>
> - Gap: Bristol Myers Squibb is not in the Filings provided, so this answer does not cover it.
> - Merck is a snapshot company, with only the FY2024 10-K (report period 2024-12-31) in scope, so no trend over time can be shown. Only the Risk Factors section was provided. The Sources do not quantify the financial effect of any of these regulatory risks.

### 6. F-8d172c8341

**Required Fact**

> In its Q2 2025 10-Q, Tesla said recent government actions have repealed or restricted consumer, manufacturing and charging infrastructure tax credits.

**Answer**

> Tesla's risk factors shifted between the FY2024 10-K and the FY2025 10-K from general production-ramp and EV-demand risks toward autonomy (Robotaxi, Cybercab), Bots, and 2025 U.S. tariff and incentive changes. The core themes of battery-cell supply, factory ramps, key-person dependence and regulation persisted [S4][S3]. The Q1 to Q3 FY2024 10-Qs only point back to the FY2023 10-K, so they show no change [S6][S7][S8].
>
> ## Coverage of the comparison
> No Coverage Gaps are listed. The FY2023 10-K is not in the Sources, so the start of the two-year window cannot be compared directly. I use the FY2024 10-K (period 2024-12-31) as the baseline and the FY2025 10-K (period 2025-12-31) as the latest filing [S4][S3].
>
> ## What was new or expanded
>
> **Autonomy and Robotaxi**
> - FY2024 framed the risk around Autopilot and FSD (Supervised) features, which "require drivers to remain fully engaged" [S4].
> - FY2025 says the company launched Robotaxi in June 2025 and plans to mass produce Cybercab, "a purpose-built Robotaxi product" [S3].
> - FY2025 adds that Robotaxi success depends on consumer acceptance of autonomous driving [S3].
> - It also notes that many existing vehicle standards "were drafted assuming the presence of a human driver" [S3].
>
> **Bots (Optimus)**
> - The FY2025 10-K has a new dedicated risk: Tesla has "yet to commercialize Bots" and cannot predict demand for them [S3].
> - The FY2024 excerpts do not mention Bots [S4].
>
> **AI compute**
> - FY2024 cited energy availability and data-center power needs [S4].
> - FY2025 adds that AI innovation demands "exponentially greater compute, memory, energy and thermal resources" that may prove insufficient in scale or affordability [S3].
>
> **Tariffs and trade**
> - The Q1 FY2025 10-Q (period 2025-03-31) added that the U.S. had announced higher tariffs and that the scope was unknown. It said retaliatory tariffs "could also adversely impact demand" [S1].
> - The FY2025 10-K says 2025 tariffs and retaliation "have impacted our supply chain costs" and "have impacted the pricing for our products" [S3].
> - The FY2024 10-K listed tariffs only among general international trade risks [S4].
>
> **Incentives**
> - The Q2 FY2025 10-Q (period 2025-06-30) added that recent governmental actions "have repealed and/or restricted consumer, manufacturing and charging infrastructure tax credits, and certain regulatory credit programs" [S2].
> - FY2024 said only that incentives "may be reduced, eliminated, amended or exhausted" [S4].
>
> **Governance and public perception**
> - The Q1 FY2025 10-Q added that criticism "has incited protests, some escalating to violence targeting our operations, products and personnel" [S1].
> - The FY2025 10-K adds a risk that the 2025 CEO Performance Award's product goals may be misaligned with consumer demand [S3].
>
> ## What stayed the same
> - Both 10-Ks cite reliance on Panasonic and CATL for battery cells [S4][S3].
> - Both cite the dependence on new factory ramps [S4][S3].
> - Both cite natural-disaster exposure at Fremont, Gigafactory Nevada and Gigafactory Shanghai [S4][S3].
> - Both cite NHTSA's Standing General Order on crash reporting [S4][S3].
> - Indebtedness rose from $7.91 billion at December 31, 2024 to $8.18 billion at December 31, 2025, as read from the Filing text [S4][S3].
> - The FY2024 10-K had a risk about failing to meet publicly announced guidance, and the FY2025 excerpts do not show it [S4][S3].
> - The FY2024 10-K carried a Russia–Ukraine conflict reference, and the FY2025 excerpts instead cite "geopolitical conflicts" generally [S4][S3].
>
> ## Coverage
> There are no listed Coverage Gaps. The Sources are excerpts, so an absence from FY2025 may reflect truncation rather than removal. The Q3 FY2025 10-Q excerpt does not list its changes [S5]. The Sources do not quantify how any risk affected financial results.

### 7. F-05f33ff6d6

**Required Fact**

> UnitedHealth identified on February 21, 2024 that cybercrime threat actors had gained access to certain Change Healthcare systems.

**Answer**

> UnitedHealth's FY2024 10-K (report period 2024-12-31) says it is regularly targeted by cyberattacks and expects to keep experiencing security incidents, some of which may hurt its business [S1]. It cites the 2024 cyberattack on its recently acquired Change Healthcare business, which involved protected health information or personally identifiable information [S1]. The later 2025 10-Qs report no material changes to these risk factors [S2][S3][S4].
>
> ## Cybersecurity risks in the FY2024 10-K (Item 1A)
>
> **Threat environment**
> - UnitedHealth says it routinely processes, stores and transmits large amounts of data, including protected personal information subject to privacy, security or data breach notification laws. Some of that data may be outside the United States [S1].
> - It says the techniques used to gain unauthorized access, degrade service or sabotage systems change frequently and are growing more sophisticated, partly through evolving AI/ML technologies including generative AI. It may therefore be unable to anticipate threats, detect incidents in time or put adequate preventive measures in place [S1].
> - Threat actors have previously penetrated, and may again penetrate, its security controls. They can disrupt or shut down systems, misappropriate information, and deploy viruses, ransomware and other malware [S1].
> - Hardware, software or applications it develops or buys from third parties may contain defects that compromise its security controls. Its systems may also be vulnerable to financial fraud schemes, misplaced or lost data, error and malicious social engineering [S1].
>
> **Acquisitions and third parties**
> - The filing says there have been and may be heightened vulnerabilities from recently acquired or non-integrated businesses [S1].
> - It describes having experienced "more acute information technology system vulnerabilities" in recently acquired businesses, and says it may be unable to fix them soon after an acquisition. That could undermine integration, delay launches of acquired products and increase infrastructure risk [S1].
> - It relies in some cases on third-party vendors to process, store and transmit large amounts of data. Those vendors face similar risks but sit outside its direct oversight and control [S1].
>
> **Consequences the filing names**
> - Loss of revenue, increased costs, negative operational effects, significant liability and reputational harm [S1].
> - Operational interruptions, delays or cessation of service, and loss of existing or potential customers, if remediation fails [S1].
> - Risk of financial or medical identity theft for affected individuals, litigation and liability including regulatory penalties, and brand damage [S1].
> - Costs to address security threats and vulnerabilities before or after an incident "could be material" [S1].
> - Data integrity failures, including in systems using AI/ML, could cause problems in setting medical cost estimates and pricing, difficulty detecting and controlling fraud, regulatory sanctions, penalties, investigations or audits, and higher operating expenses [S1].
>
> **Mitigation described**
> - UnitedHealth says it has programs to detect, contain and respond to data security incidents, and gives employees training on phishing, malware and other cyber threats. It also maintains, updates and regularly tests business continuation and resiliency plans [S1].
>
> **Related compliance exposure**
> - The filing says that failing to comply with privacy, security, technology and data laws, including for third-party providers using protected personal information on its behalf, could materially harm its business, reputation, results and cash flows. It adds that incidents could bring significant fines, penalties and litigation awards [S1].
> - Uncertain and evolving U.S. federal and state, non-U.S. and international laws on health data and health information technologies, including those using AI/ML, could impose new compliance requirements [S1].
>
> ## Coverage
> - Coverage Gaps: none.
> - Sources used: the FY2024 10-K, the latest annual report in scope (report period 2024-12-31), plus the Q1, Q2 and Q3 FY2025 10-Qs (report periods 2025-03-31, 2025-06-30 and 2025-09-30), which only state that the risk factors are unchanged.
> - The Sources do not give the Change Healthcare incident's financial cost, the number of people affected, or details of the cybersecurity governance disclosure (Item 1C). The excerpts do not include those items.

### 8. F-1ff8dbc37d

**Required Fact**

> Tesla says it has faced, and expects to continue to face, claims and regulatory scrutiny related to its driver assistance and autonomous driving features.

**Answer**

> **JPMorgan Chase appears to have the broadest ongoing regulatory exposure in these disclosures**, because its filing describes extensive supervision across the jurisdictions where it operates and regulatory requirements that can directly constrain its products, pricing, liquidity, assets and business activities. This is a qualitative comparison of the risks described—not a quantified measure of exposure—and the periods differ: Apple’s filings are its 10-K for FY2025 (report period September 27, 2025) and 10-Q for Q1 FY2026 (December 27, 2025), while Tesla’s and JPMorgan Chase’s are 10-Ks for FY2025 (both report period December 31, 2025). [S1] [S2] [S3] [S4]
>
> ### Comparison
>
> | Company | Regulatory exposure described in the filing |
> |---|---|
> | **JPMorgan Chase** | The filing says JPMorgan Chase is subject to extensive supervision and regulation that significantly affects how it conducts business and structures operations, and must comply with applicable law in every jurisdiction where it does business. Changes in law, regulatory decisions or supervisory expectations could require it to limit products and services, raise prices, reduce liquidity provided through market-making, pay higher taxes or charges, dispose of assets, or forgo opportunities; these changes could increase costs or reduce revenue. [S4] |
> | **Apple** | Apple identifies a wide range of changing worldwide requirements, including antitrust, privacy and data security, online safety, age verification, digital platforms, AI, trade, labor, tax and environmental rules. Its filing gives concrete examples: in the EU it has changed iOS, iPadOS, the App Store and Safari to comply with the Digital Markets Act, including alternative app distribution, payment processing and fee structures; the Commission has challenged its compliance plan, and the DMA provides for significant fines. In the U.S., Apple says a court order prevents it from imposing a commission or fee on certain consumer purchases. [S1] |
> | **Tesla** | Tesla describes exposure to evolving rules across vehicle, energy and data businesses. Driver-assistance features, future autonomous capability and its Robotaxi business may be affected by international, federal and state regulations, including vehicle standards written on the assumption of a human driver; changing or conflicting rules could restrict functionality or delay Robotaxi expansion. Tesla also identifies environmental, occupational, health and safety requirements, foreign vehicle safety, fuel-economy and emissions standards, and privacy laws including the EU General Data Protection Regulation and California Consumer Privacy Act. [S3] |
>
> JPMorgan Chase is the clearest answer if “most” means the breadth and continuing reach of supervision over the company’s operations: the filing describes regulatory oversight as a feature of its business across jurisdictions, and notes that examinations can lead to investigations, enforcement actions, penalties and remediation costs. It also identifies regulatory capital requirements that can restrict share repurchases, dividends, executive bonuses or business activity. [S4] Apple and Tesla, however, disclose substantial and specific regulatory exposure in their own areas—particularly Apple’s platform-related obligations and Tesla’s vehicle, autonomous-driving and energy rules—so the filings do not establish a numerical ranking of total exposure or likely costs. [S1] [S3] [S4]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> Coverage gap: periods differ—the Companies’ latest Filings here cover different periods (Apple: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-09-27; Tesla: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-12-31; JPMorgan Chase: 10-K FY2025, year to 2025-12-31). The sources do not provide a common quantitative measure of regulatory exposure, or enough information to compare expected regulatory costs, penalties or outcomes across the three companies. [S1] [S2] [S3] [S4]

### 9. F-3673bc8ee1

**Required Fact**

> Tesla says it has faced, and expects to continue to face, claims and regulatory scrutiny related to its driver assistance and autonomous driving features.

**Answer**

> JPMorgan Chase appears to have the broadest and most directly embedded regulatory exposure in these filings: it describes extensive supervision and regulation affecting how it conducts business and structures operations, as well as possible restrictions on products, pricing, market-making, assets and business activities. The filings do not provide a common measure that would establish which company has the greatest exposure quantitatively, and their reporting periods differ. [S4]
>
> ### Apple
>
> - Apple’s FY2025 10-K, for the year ended September 27, 2025, describes worldwide legal and regulatory exposure spanning antitrust, privacy, data security and localization, online safety and age verification, digital platforms, AI, labor, trade, tax and environmental rules; it says compliance is onerous and expensive and may increase costs or require changes to products, services or supply chains. [S1]
> - The filing gives concrete examples of business changes: Apple implemented changes to developer communications about alternative purchasing mechanisms in the U.S. App Store and is subject to a court order preventing certain commissions or fees; in the EU it changed iOS, iPadOS, the App Store and Safari to address the Digital Markets Act, including alternative distribution, payment processing and fee structures, and says its compliance plan has been challenged by the Commission. [S1]
> - Apple’s Q1 FY2026 10-Q, for the quarter ended December 27, 2025, reports no material changes to the FY2025 10-K risk factors. [S2]
>
> ### Tesla
>
> - Tesla’s FY2025 10-K, for the year ended December 31, 2025, identifies regulation across environmental, occupational, health and safety requirements; vehicle safety, fuel economy and emissions; privacy; direct vehicle sales; and energy-system imports, grid interconnection and electricity sales. [S3]
> - Its filing identifies specific exposure to changing incentives: recent governmental and regulatory actions repealed or restricted consumer, manufacturing and charging-infrastructure tax credits and certain regulatory credit programs, while OBBBA provisions could affect battery-cell expenses and consumer costs; insufficient critical-mineral origin documentation could also cost products eligibility for tax credits and incentives. [S3]
> - Tesla also says changing rules for driver-assistance features and autonomous capability may restrict designs or functionality or slow its Robotaxi expansion, and differing foreign vehicle standards can require additional investment and regulatory review before market entry. [S3]
>
> ### JPMorgan Chase
>
> - JPMorgan Chase’s FY2025 10-K, for the year ended December 31, 2025, presents supervision and regulation as a central exposure: changing law, interpretations, supervisory expectations or executive actions could require business modifications, raise costs or reduce revenues, while noncompliance could bring scrutiny, enforcement, penalties, litigation or reputational harm. [S4]
> - It specifically warns that regulators could require it to limit products, raise prices, reduce market-making liquidity, pay higher taxes or charges, dispose of assets, forgo opportunities or otherwise restrict activities; larger firms may face more stringent oversight, while less-regulated competitors and differences in international implementation may put it at a disadvantage. [S4]
> - The filing also describes recurring examinations and potentially overlapping proceedings across U.S. and non-U.S. authorities, with past penalties and remediation costs, and says capital requirements can restrict buybacks, dividends, executive bonuses or business activity. [S4]
>
> **Coverage:** Periods differ: Apple’s latest filing here is its 10-K FY2025, year ended September 27, 2025; Tesla’s and JPMorgan Chase’s are 10-K FY2025 filings, each for the year ended December 31, 2025. [S1][S3][S4] The Sources do not quantify comparable regulatory exposure or establish a definitive measured ranking; Apple’s Q1 FY2026 10-Q provides no material risk-factor changes. [S2]

### 10. F-b419393ab0

**Required Fact**

> AMD recorded inventory and related charges on its MI308 GPUs in the second quarter of fiscal 2025 because of new U.S. export restrictions to China.

**Answer**

> **The Sources do not identify any company that took charges because of new U.S. export controls on products sold to China.** The only filings provided are Apple's, and they describe export restrictions and tariffs as risks, not as charges Apple recorded [S1][S2]. The question also asks about "companies" in general, and no other company's filings are in the Sources, so nothing can be said about any other company.
>
> ## Apple Inc (AAPL)
>
> What Apple's 10-K for FY2025 (report period 2025-09-27) says about trade and export restrictions:
>
> - **Export controls are listed only as a risk.** Restrictions on international trade can "materially adversely affect the Company's business and supply chain." These include "tariffs and other controls on imports or exports of goods, technology or data" [S1]. Such measures can:
>   - raise the cost or limit the availability of products, components, "rare earths and other raw materials"
>   - force Apple to change suppliers, stop offering affected products and services, or raise prices [S1]
> - **Changing regulations on the export and import of its products.** Apple says its programs "may not be effective in preventing a violation or a claim of a violation," and its products "could be banned, delayed or prohibited from importation" [S1].
> - **U.S. Tariffs.** "Beginning in the second quarter of 2025, new tariffs were announced on imports to the U.S." These include additional tariffs on imports from China, India, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan, Vietnam and the EU, and other countries have imposed or threatened reciprocal tariffs [S1].
>   - The U.S. Department of Commerce has opened a Section 232 investigation under the Trade Expansion Act of 1962 into imports of semiconductors and products containing them [S1].
>   - Apple calls the ultimate impact "uncertain" [S1].
> - **China exposure.** A significant majority of manufacturing is done by outsourcing partners located primarily in China mainland, India, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan and Vietnam [S1].
> - **Write-down risk, but no charge disclosed.** Apple says it may incur inventory write-downs, impairments and other charges "given the rapid and unpredictable pace of product obsolescence." It ties this to obsolescence, not to export controls, and discloses no specific charge [S1].
>
> Apple's 10-Q for Q1 FY2026 (report period 2025-12-27) reports "no material changes" to its risk factors since the 2025 Form 10-K [S2].
>
> ## Coverage
>
> - **Coverage Gaps:** none listed.
> - **Filings used:** Apple 10-K FY2025 (report period 2025-09-27) and Apple 10-Q Q1 FY2026 (report period 2025-12-27).
> - **What the Sources could not answer:**
>   - They contain only Apple's risk factor sections. There is no financial statement, notes or MD&A text that would show whether Apple recorded any charge tied to export controls.
>   - They contain no filings from any other company, so they cannot show which other companies may have taken such charges.
> - **What would help:** export-control charges would more likely appear in income statement notes or MD&A, for example as inventory write-downs or purchase-commitment charges. Filings from the companies of interest would be needed to answer the question.

### 12. F-4dc8219c33

**Required Fact**

> Merck says HHS selected Januvia for the first year of IRA price setting, with a government-set price effective January 1, 2026.

**Answer**

> The five major pharmaceutical companies, as listed in the Sector table, are Johnson & Johnson, Eli Lilly, Merck, Pfizer and AbbVie. All five name government pricing programs, patent challenges and drug-approval and manufacturing regulation as risks [S1][S2][S3][S4][S8]. Their filings say little about how they respond: they mostly mention monitoring, defending patents and taking legal action [S1][S2][S4][S8]. The filings cover different periods, so this is not a comparison of the same period.
>
> ### Johnson & Johnson (10-K FY2025, period to 2025-12-28)
> - **Patent challenges:** Competitors use inter partes review (IPR) proceedings at the USPTO, and generic or biosimilar makers may launch "at-risk." This can cost "a major portion of revenues" quickly [S1].
> - **FDA capacity:** Reductions to the U.S. FDA's budget, staff and operations may slow reviews of new products [S1].
> - **Tax:** Changes such as global minimum taxes are a risk. The company says it "closely monitors these proposals" and regularly assesses its tax audits and reserves [S1].
> - **Trade and data rules:** Tariffs, possible reciprocal tariffs and data localization laws are named, as are new AI laws that may "limit our ability to continue the use of these technologies" [S1].
> - **Orthopaedics separation:** The planned separation, announced in October 2025, needs regulatory approvals [S1].
>
> ### Eli Lilly (10-K FY2025, period to 2025-12-31)
> - **Pricing:**
>   - Lilly agreed with the U.S. government to lower Medicaid and certain other drug prices; it notes the risk of unfavorable terms in the definitive agreements [S2].
>   - HHS selected Jardiance for government-set Medicare prices taking effect in 2026 [S2].
> - **Competition review of deals:** The FTC and European authorities scrutinize business development transactions [S2].
> - **Marketing and policy programs:** The filing cites limits on direct-to-patient advertising and risks from taking part in the FDA National Priority Voucher Pilot Program [S2].
> - **Compounded and counterfeit incretins:** Lilly's actions to stop illegal sales "are costly and may be ineffective." It has filed lawsuits to protect its IP [S2].
> - **Response on access:** It is expanding self-pay access through LillyDirect [S2].
> - **Manufacturing quality:** cGMP scrutiny, including of nitrosamines [S2].
>
> ### Merck (10-K FY2024, period to 2024-12-31)
> - **Pricing:** Pressure comes from the Medicare Prescription Drug Improvement and Modernization Act of 2003, the ACA, the IRA and state price-transparency laws [S3].
> - **Tariffs:** Planned U.S. tariff increases, particularly on Canada and Mexico, plus tariffs already raised on China. Merck says it is too early to assess their effect [S3].
> - **Climate rules:** Climate regulation could bring carbon pricing [S3].
> - **Response:**
>   - Merck seeks patents in each market where it plans to sell [S3].
>   - It self-insures substantially all product liability risk [S3].
>
> ### Pfizer (10-K FY2024, period to 2024-12-31; Q1–Q3 FY2025 10-Qs)
> - **U.S. pricing:**
>   - The IRA [S4].
>   - Medicare's average-sales-price reimbursement, which pressures its biosimilars [S4].
>   - Drug importation bills and prescription drug affordability boards (PDABs) [S4].
> - **Other markets:** China's QCE and VBP tenders have cut off-patent prices sharply [S4].
> - **EU reform:** The EU pharmaceutical reform, amended by the European Parliament in April 2024, could alter exclusivity periods [S4].
> - **EU HTA-R (effective January 2025):** Pfizer is "engaging and monitoring" how it is applied [S4].
> - **Manufacturing:** cGMP inspections can lead to warning letters or import bans [S4].
> - **Oxbryta:** Pfizer voluntarily withdrew it in September 2024 [S4].
> - **Compulsory licensing:** The WTO's June 2022 decision makes compulsory licenses on COVID-19 vaccines easier [S4].
> - **2025 10-Qs:** These only refer back to the 2024 10-K risk factors and add no new text [S5][S6][S7].
>
> ### AbbVie (10-K FY2024, period to 2024-12-31)
> - **Patent challenges:** Generic makers file ANDAs, and petitioners use inter partes review and post grant review under the 2011 Leahy-Smith America Invents Act [S8].
> - **Compulsory licenses:** Governments outside the U.S. may seek them [S8].
> - **Humira:** Biosimilars have competed with Humira since its 2023 loss of exclusivity [S8].
> - **Biologics:** They face more complex regulation than other drugs [S8].
> - **Anti-corruption laws:** The Foreign Corrupt Practices Act and the UK Bribery Act [S8].
> - **Response:** AbbVie

### 13. F-078be9810b

**Required Fact**

> Tesla's 2023 net income included a one-time non-cash tax benefit for the release of valuation allowance on certain deferred tax assets.

**Answer**

> Tesla’s net income attributable to common stockholders fell sharply over FY2023–FY2025: from **$14,997 million** in FY2023 to **$7,091 million** in FY2024 and **$3,794 million** in FY2025. The reported year-over-year declines were **52.7%** in FY2024 and **46.5%** in FY2025. [S4] [S8] [S12]
>
> The annual figures are from Tesla’s 10-K filings for FY2023 (report period December 31, 2023), FY2024 (December 31, 2024) and FY2025 (December 31, 2025); the quarterly detail below is from 10-Q filings for the stated quarter-end report periods. [S4] [S8] [S12] [S1] [S2] [S3] [S5] [S6] [S7] [S9] [S10] [S11]
>
> | Fiscal period | Net income attributable to common stockholders |
> |---|---:|
> | FY2023 | $14,997 million [S4] |
> | FY2024 | $7,091 million; down 52.7% from FY2023 [S8] |
> | FY2025 | $3,794 million; down 46.5% from FY2024 [S12] |
>
> ### Direction within each year
>
> The quarterly figures show that FY2024’s earnings were below the corresponding FY2023 quarters in Q1 and Q2, but higher in Q3: **$1,129 million** in Q1 FY2024 versus **$2,513 million** in Q1 FY2023, **$1,478 million** versus **$2,703 million** in Q2, and **$2,167 million** versus **$1,853 million** in Q3. [S5] [S1] [S6] [S2] [S7] [S3] FY2024 Q4 net income was **$2,317 million**, derived from FY2024’s $7,091 million less Q1–Q3; that was **70.8% lower** than derived FY2023 Q4 net income of **$7,928 million**. [S8] [S5] [S6] [S7] [S4] [S1] [S2] [S3]
>
> In FY2025, net income was below the prior-year quarter in each of the first three quarters: **$409 million** in Q1, **$1,172 million** in Q2 and **$1,373 million** in Q3. [S9] [S10] [S11] Q4 FY2025 was **$840 million**, derived from FY2025’s $3,794 million less Q1–Q3; derived Q4 was **63.7% lower** year over year. [S12] [S9] [S10] [S11] [S8] [S5] [S6] [S7]
>
> ### Filing commentary relevant to the decline
>
> Tesla attributed the FY2024 change in income taxes in part to releasing the valuation allowance on U.S. deferred tax assets in Q4 FY2023; the company reported its effective tax rate changed from a **50% benefit in FY2023** to a **20% expense in FY2024**. [S36] For FY2025, Tesla said its income tax provision decreased primarily because income before taxes was lower, while its effective tax rate increased from **20% to 27%**, citing jurisdictional earnings mix, lower foreign income deductions associated with the OBBBA, and remeasurement of deferred tax assets related to NCTI under the OBBBA. [S35] It also reported an unfavorable **$1.11 billion** change in other (expense) income, net, in FY2025, primarily due to bitcoin digital-asset mark-to-market and foreign-exchange fluctuations on intercompany balances. [S35]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> Coverage gaps: **None.** The filings provide annual and quarterly net income figures and the commentary cited above; they do not, in the excerpts provided, give a complete quantified reconciliation of the annual net-income declines.

### 14. F-59a4087f2b

**Required Fact**

> Merck says HHS selected Januvia for the first year of the IRA's price setting program, with a government set price effective January 1, 2026.

**Answer**

> Merck faces regulatory risks involving U.S. drug pricing and reimbursement rules, product approvals and safety oversight, and evolving requirements across the jurisdictions where it operates. Bristol Myers Squibb is not covered because it is not in the Filings provided, so the sources do not support a comparison of the two companies’ risks. [S1]
>
> ### Merck & Co. Inc. (MRK)
>
> The source is Merck’s **10-K for FY2024**, with a **report period ending 2024-12-31**. [S1]
>
> - **U.S. drug-pricing and reimbursement changes:** Merck says the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA) creates penalties for drugs whose prices rise faster than inflation, shifts more Medicare Part D liability to manufacturers (effective in 2025), and provides for government price setting for certain Part D drugs from 2026 and Part B drugs from 2028. Merck identifies Januvia as selected for a government-set price effective January 1, 2026; Janumet and Janumet XR as selected for a price effective January 1, 2027; and says it expects Keytruda to be selected in 2026 for a price effective January 1, 2028, after which it expects U.S. Keytruda sales to decline. [S1]
> - **Other U.S. pricing and discount exposure:** Merck identifies the removal, beginning in January 2024, of the statutory cap on Medicaid rebates as a risk for drugs with high Medicaid use and rebate exposure above 100% of Average Manufacturer Price. It also says increased use of the 340B Federal Drug Discount Program and limits on identifying inappropriate discounts are negatively affecting performance. Merck expects states to continue pursuing pharmaceutical pricing measures, including Prescription Drug Affordability Boards that may conduct affordability reviews and set upper payment limits. [S1]
> - **Approvals, oversight and post-approval action:** Merck’s research, clinical trials, manufacturing and marketing are subject to extensive U.S. and foreign regulation, including FDA oversight and authorities in the EU, Japan and China. Regulators may require additional testing, delay or withhold approval, or preclude distribution; Merck cannot market products in a jurisdiction without obtaining and maintaining required approvals. After approval, Phase 4 results, safety reviews, recalls, changing standards, scrutiny of promotion, or withdrawal of accelerated-approval indications may reduce demand or constrain sales. [S1]
> - **Potential policy changes:** Merck says publicly discussed U.S. policy changes could affect vaccines and vaccine development, FDA and other agency personnel and policies, and CDC vaccine recommendations. It also notes that eliminating FDA drug-user-fee programs could significantly delay facility inspections and new-product approvals; it says it cannot predict which proposed changes will be implemented. [S1]
> - **Other changing legal requirements:** Merck identifies possible additional U.S. or international health-care reform discounts or fees; laws affecting pricing, reimbursement, access and marketing; data-privacy rules, particularly in the EU, U.S. and China; reporting of payments to health-care professionals; local-manufacturing mandates or preferences; and sustainability requirements such as the EU’s Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive. It says compliance costs or non-compliance could adversely affect its business. [S1]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> **Coverage Gap:** “Not in the Corpus: Bristol Myers Squibb is not in the Filings provided, so this answer does not cover it.” Merck is covered by one FY2024 10-K, so these sources cannot establish regulatory-risk developments across multiple filing periods; they also cannot describe Bristol Myers Squibb’s risks. [S1]

LABEL F-59a4087f2b (stated / not stated):

### 15. F-fbf1325c1a

**Required Fact**

> GE Capital says its strategy is to exit businesses that are non-strategic or underperforming.

**Answer**

> GE Capital’s FY2014 Form 10-K (report period December 31, 2014) described reducing its ending net investment (ENI), excluding liquidity, as a deliberate balance-sheet reduction strategy, with a target of less than $300 billion. GE Capital said the strategy involved concentrating on selected financial-services products and exiting non-core or underperforming businesses; it warned that the exits could reduce near-term earnings, while expecting longer-term benefits from higher returns, lower liquidity risk and stronger capital ratios. [S1]
>
> ### Strategy and target
>
> - GE Capital reported ENI, excluding liquidity, of $363 billion at December 31, 2014, and said it had communicated a goal of reducing that balance to less than $300 billion. It defined ENI as a measure of capital invested in the financial-services business and said excluding liquidity made it a meaningful indicator of assets requiring capital to fund the segment. [S1]
> - To pursue the target, the company said it would focus more aggressively on financial-services products where it had “deep domain experience,” broad distribution, consistent returns on capital and competitive advantages, while managing overall balance-sheet size and risk. It also said it planned to exit businesses considered non-strategic or underperforming. [S1]
>
> ### Trade-offs and expected effects
>
> - GE Capital cautioned that, in the short term, reducing ENI by exiting non-core businesses could lower net earnings and potentially involve impairments, restructuring and other non-cash charges. [S1]
> - Over the longer term, management expected the strategy to improve performance through higher returns by concentrating assets in core businesses, reduce liquidity risk by paying down debt and diversifying funding sources, and reduce capital requirements while strengthening capital ratios. The filing specifically cited less reliance on global commercial-paper markets and greater use of alternative funding sources such as deposits. [S1]
> - The filing described the aim as becoming a “smaller, more focused finance company”; it also reported that ENI, excluding liquidity, had been reduced to $363 billion at year-end 2014. [S1]
>
> ### Examples of portfolio and funding actions
>
> - GE Capital said it completed the sale of GE Money Bank AB, its consumer-finance business in Sweden, Denmark and Norway, to Santander in the fourth quarter of 2014, for proceeds of $2.3 billion; it had also signed an agreement to sell Budapest Bank to Hungary’s government. [S1][S7]
> - Its 2015 funding plan anticipated repaying principal on outstanding short-term borrowings, including the current portion of long-term debt ($38.0 billion at December 31, 2014), using long-term debt issuance and commercial-paper reissuance, cash, dispositions, asset sales, deposits and other alternative funding. [S1]
>
> ### Coverage
>
> The stated coverage gap is: “Stale Filing: the only General Electric Filing is GE Capital's 10-K for FY2014 (Report Period 2014-12-31); it does not describe General Electric today, so it is cited here only as GE Capital's report for FY2014.” The cited filing is a 10-K for FY2014, report period December 31, 2014; it does not establish GE Capital’s current balance-sheet strategy or current progress against the target. [S1]

These and other impacts can materially adversely affect the Company’s business, results of operations, financial condition and stock price.The Company’s business can be impacted by political events, trade and other international disputes, geopolitical tensions, conflict, terrorism, natural disasters, public health issues, industrial accidents and other business interruptions.Political events, trade and other international disputes, geopolitical tensions, conflict, terrorism, natural disasters, public health issues, industrial accidents and other business interruptions can have a material adverse effect on the Company and its customers, employees, suppliers, contract manufacturers, logistics providers, distributors, cellular network carriers and other channel partners.The Company has a large, global business with sales outside the U.S. representing a majority of the Company’s total net sales, and the Company believes that it generally benefits from growth in international trade. A significant majority of the Company’s manufacturing is performed in whole or in part by outsourcing partners located primarily in China mainland, India, Japan, South Korea, Taiwan and Vietnam, in addition to sourcing from partners and facilities located in the U.S. Restrictions on international trade, such as tariffs and other controls on imports or exports of goods, technology or data, can materially adversely affect the Company’s business and supply chain. The impact can be particularly significant if these restrictive measures apply to countries and regions where the Company derives a significant portion of its revenues and/or has significant supply chain operations. Restrictive measures can also require the Company to change suppliers, restructure business relationships and operations, refrain from offering and distributing or cease to offer and distribute affected products, services and third-party applications to its customers, and increase the prices of its products and services. Changing the Company’s business and supply chain in accordance with new or changed restrictions on international trade can be expensive, time-consuming and disruptive to the Company’s business and results of operations. Trade and other international disputes can also have an adverse impact on the overall macroeconomic environment and result in shifts and reductions in consumer spending and negative consumer sentiment for the Company’s products and services, all of which can further adversely affect the Company’s business and results of operations. In response, several countries have imposed, or threatened to impose, reciprocal tariffs on imports from the U.S. and other retaliatory measures. Various modifications to the U.S. Tariffs have been announced and further changes could be made in the future, which may include additional sector-based tariffs or other measures. For example, the U.S. Department of Commerce has initiated an investigation under Section 232 of the Trade Expansion Act of 1962, as amended, into, among other things, imports of semiconductors, semiconductor manufacturing equipment, and their derivative products, including downstream products that contain semiconductors. The ultimate impact remains uncertain and will depend on several factors, including whether additional or incremental U.S. Tariffs or other measures are announced or imposed, to what extent other countries implement tariffs or other retaliatory measures in response, and the overall magnitude and duration of these measures.
